Lately I just kind of sit there and come up with excuses not to go, because none of it feels like it’s worth the effort. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work or not thinking about your manager, do you still feel this same heavy, hollow mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. I wish I could say it’s only when I’m dealing with him or thinking about work, but it’s there even when I’m home. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like… like the old Ruben, the guy who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy the day, is just kind of gone. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so, not like this. I mean, everybody has rough stretches, sure, but nothing I can remember that lasted weeks and felt this heavy. At least not that I’d want to call the same thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this down even on days when work is going well or isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even then. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. Like the old Ruben—the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy the day—is just… not really there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sleep’s been off. I mean, I still get some sleep, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m up all night every night, but a lot of nights I’m lying there staring at the ceiling, replaying work stuff in my head. And then when I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning already feeling worn out, kind of dreading the day. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I can still get through things when I have to, so I don’t want to say I can’t concentrate if that’s not accurate. Maybe I’m a little slower sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe a little, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I mean, I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really been tracking my energy, I guess. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I’m not sure if that’s unusual or just… life, you know? Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. I mean, I still eat. Maybe some days I’m not that interested, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a real change. Should I be paying closer attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you’ve let people down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… yes, I guess I do. I keep telling myself I’m kind of useless now. Like all those years taking care of clients and providing for my family don’t really count if I can’t even handle this job anymore. That sounds bad saying it out loud, but… that’s been in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want to worry anyone, and I’m not saying I’ve made some plan or anything like that. But… yeah, I have had thoughts like maybe my wife and kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nIt scares me to even say that. I don’t like admitting it. I guess I don’t trust myself the way I used to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking you might act on those thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think I want to act on them. I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer that right.\n\nIt’s more that the thoughts come up, like maybe they’d be better off if I didn’t wake up, and that scares me. Enough that… I don’t feel like I trust myself the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the old Ruben—the guy who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy his day—is just… gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or high, more than your usual self?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high or energized? I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had good days here and there like anybody, or times I try to push through and act more upbeat for work, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt, you know, “too” energized or not like myself in that way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about eight months or so. It kind of started after the new regional manager came in and started, um, really coming down on me—taking away accounts I’d handled for years. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming everything on work, but that’s around when I noticed I wasn’t feeling right.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or anything, but things I used to really look forward to don’t do much for me now. Bowling, working out in the garden, taking my wife to those gallery shows she likes… we used to go dancing sometimes, too. Lately I just kind of sit there and come up with excuses not to go, because none of it feels like it’s worth the effort. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work or not thinking about your manager, do you still feel this same heavy, hollow mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. I wish I could say it’s only when I’m dealing with him or thinking about work, but it’s there even when I’m home. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest, like… like the old Ruben, the guy who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy the day, is just kind of gone. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so, not like this. I mean, everybody has rough stretches, sure, but nothing I can remember that lasted weeks and felt this heavy. At least not that I’d want to call the same thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this down even on days when work is going well or isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even then. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. Like the old Ruben—the one who could talk to anybody and actually enjoy the day—is just… not really there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my sleep’s been off. I mean, I still get some sleep, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m up all night every night, but a lot of nights I’m lying there staring at the ceiling, replaying work stuff in my head. And then when I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around three or four in the morning already feeling worn out, kind of dreading the day. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I can still get through things when I have to, so I don’t want to say I can’t concentrate if that’s not accurate. Maybe I’m a little slower sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe a little, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I mean, I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really been tracking my energy, I guess. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I’m not sure if that’s unusual or just… life, you know? I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming everything on work, but that’s around when I noticed I wasn’t feeling right.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or anything, but things I used to really look forward to don’t do much for me now. I don’t know if I’m explaining that right.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so, not like this. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I can still get through things when I have to, so I don’t want to say I can’t concentrate if that’s not accurate. Maybe I’m a little slower sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe a little, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. I don’t think so, no. I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or anything, but things I used to really look forward to don’t do much for me now. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean… I haven’t really thought of it that way. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.
